People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… mostly I just don’t feel like myself anymore. That’s the simplest way to say it, though it doesn’t really explain it.

I used to be active, you know? I’d go hiking, I coached, I played guitar, I’d keep up with people—call them back, check in, make plans. And now it’s like all of that just sort of… stopped. Not because I decided to stop or because I don’t know those things matter. I’ll think, “I should pick up the guitar,” or “I should call him back,” or “I need to get this done,” and then I just sit there.

Some days I’m in that small rental place for hours, literally hours, and I know there are things I should be doing—simple things, not even big things—and I can’t seem to make myself start. It’s like the part of me that used to get up and move is just missing or stuck or something. It’s been bothering me because I don’t recognize myself like this.

I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. That’s the part that’s hard to explain. It’s not like I’m crying all day or walking around thinking, “I’m miserable,” necessarily.

Empty… maybe closer, but even that word feels a little dramatic. It’s more like things don’t land the way they used to. Like I can recognize that something should make me feel something, but I’m sort of sitting behind glass watching it happen. So I’m not sure if that counts as sad or empty most of the time. I just know I don’t feel like myself.

Not because I decided to stop or because I don’t know those things matter. I’ll think, “I should pick up the guitar,” or “I should call him back,” or “I need to get this done,” and then I just sit there.\n\nSome days I’m in that small rental place for hours, literally hours, and I know there are things I should be doing—simple things, not even big things—and I can’t seem to make myself start. It’s like the part of me that used to get up and move is just missing or stuck or something. It’s been bothering me because I don’t recognize myself like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. It’s more like things don’t land the way they used to. Like I can recognize that something should make me feel something, but I’m sort of sitting behind glass watching it happen. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean… I still know I used to enjoy them. That’s the weird part. If you asked me, “Do you like hiking?” I’d still say yes, because that’s who I was, you know? I used to be the guy who’d get up early, pack a bag, find a trail, make a whole day of it. Or I’d sit with the guitar for an hour without even thinking about it. Coaching too—I cared about that. I liked being useful, being around people, having something to show up for.

But now it’s like there’s no pull toward any of it. I look at the guitar and I don’t feel excited. I think about going hiking and it feels like this huge impossible thing, even though I know it used to make me feel better. And calling people back… I know that’s not exactly “pleasure,” but staying connected used to feel normal to me, and now even that feels like work I can’t start.

Some days I just sit in my rental for hours, knowing there are things I could do—things I used to want to do—and I just don’t move toward them. It’s not that I hate them now. It’s more like the enjoyment got disconnected from the idea of doing them.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Guilty… I don’t know. Not in the way I think you mean, maybe.

I do think a lot about the things I’m not doing. Like I’ll sit there and think, “You should’ve called him back,” or “You should’ve gotten up and taken care of that,” or “You’re letting things pile up.” So there’s some self-criticism in that, I guess. I don’t feel great about it. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because these are basic things, and I used to be dependable.

But I don’t know if I’d describe it as this heavy guilt, like I did something terrible. It’s more like frustration with myself, or confusion. Like I can see the gap between who I was and what I’m actually doing now, and I don’t know how to explain it. So yes, I feel bad about not doing things, but I’m not sure “guilty” is the exact word.

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been… a while. I can’t give you the exact day, but if I trace it back honestly, things started coming apart after I moved into that smaller rental place after the divorce.

That was about a year and two months ago now, give or take. At first I told myself it was just the adjustment, you know—new place, different routine, everything feeling kind of stripped down. I thought maybe once I settled in, I’d get back to normal. But it didn’t really go that way. It’s more like things slowly kept unraveling from there, and by the time I noticed how far off I’d gotten, it had already been months.

So… yeah, around fourteen months, I guess. Since that move.

I’ll think, “I should pick up the guitar,” or “I should call him back,” or “I need to get this done,” and then I just sit there.\n\nSome days I’m in that small rental place for hours, literally hours, and I know there are things I should be doing—simple things, not even big things—and I can’t seem to make myself start. It’s like the part of me that used to get up and move is just missing or stuck or something. It’s been bothering me because I don’t recognize myself like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. That’s the part that’s hard to explain. It’s not like I’m crying all day or walking around thinking, “I’m miserable,” necessarily.\n\nEmpty… maybe closer, but even that word feels a little dramatic. It’s more like things don’t land the way they used to. Like I can recognize that something should make me feel something, but I’m sort of sitting behind glass watching it happen. So I’m not sure if that counts as sad or empty most of the time. I just know I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I still know I used to enjoy them. That’s the weird part. If you asked me, “Do you like hiking?” I’d still say yes, because that’s who I was, you know? I used to be the guy who’d get up early, pack a bag, find a trail, make a whole day of it. Or I’d sit with the guitar for an hour without even thinking about it. Coaching too—I cared about that. I liked being useful, being around people, having something to show up for.\n\nBut now it’s like there’s no pull toward any of it. I look at the guitar and I don’t feel excited. I think about going hiking and it feels like this huge impossible thing, even though I know it used to make me feel better. And calling people back… I know that’s not exactly “pleasure,” but staying connected used to feel normal to me, and now even that feels like work I can’t start.\n\nSome days I just sit in my rental for hours, knowing there are things I could do—things I used to want to do—and I just don’t move toward them. It’s not that I hate them now. It’s more like the enjoyment got disconnected from the idea of doing them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. Not in the way I think you mean, maybe.\n\nI do think a lot about the things I’m not doing. Like I’ll sit there and think, “You should’ve called him back,” or “You should’ve gotten up and taken care of that,” or “You’re letting things pile up.” So there’s some self-criticism in that, I guess. I don’t feel great about it. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because these are basic things, and I used to be dependable.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d describe it as this heavy guilt, like I did something terrible. It’s more like frustration with myself, or confusion. Like I can see the gap between who I was and what I’m actually doing now, and I don’t know how to explain it. So yes, I feel bad about not doing things, but I’m not sure “guilty” is the exact word.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. I can’t give you the exact day, but if I trace it back honestly, things started coming apart after I moved into that smaller rental place after the divorce.\n\nThat was about a year and two months ago now, give or take. At first I told myself it was just the adjustment, you know—new place, different routine, everything feeling kind of stripped down. I thought maybe once I settled in, I’d get back to normal. But it didn’t really go that way. It’s more like things slowly kept unraveling from there, and by the time I noticed how far off I’d gotten, it had already been months.\n\nSo… yeah, around fourteen months, I guess. Since that move.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything—not like I suddenly had to buy new pants or someone pointed it out to me.\n\nI probably don’t pay as much attention as I should. Some days I’ll catch myself in the mirror and think I look… I don’t know, worn down maybe, but that’s not the same as weight. I haven’t stepped on a scale recently enough to give you a real number. So if you mean, like, a clear significant gain or loss, I haven’t noticed one.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say my body is literally slower, like someone watching me would say I’m moving in slow motion. But everything feels like it takes so much more effort than it used to. Starting things is the hardest part.

I’ll be sitting in the rental, and I know there are things I need to do—laundry, dishes, returning a call, going outside, whatever—and I’ll just sit there for hours. Not because I’m enjoying resting. It doesn’t feel restful. It’s more like I can’t get myself to shift into motion. Like there’s this big gap between knowing what needs to happen and actually making my body do it.

That’s the part that doesn’t feel like me. I used to be active. I used to hike. I coached. I played guitar. I kept up with people better. Now even picking up the phone to call someone back can feel like this ridiculous mountain. And then I feel stupid because it’s not like anyone is asking me to do something impossible. It’s basic stuff.

So… yes, physical things feel harder, but maybe not just physical. It’s like everything requires this push I don’t have anymore.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly. It’s not even that I’m walking around feeling sad every second, exactly. It’s more like the things that used to make me feel like me just don’t seem to reach me anymore.

I used to hike a lot. Not professionally or anything, just… getting out on a trail, being outside, having my head clear for a while. And I coached, too, which used to give me a real sense of purpose. I liked showing up, being useful, being around people in that structured kind of way. I played guitar. I’d call people back, keep up with friends, that sort of thing. Those weren’t chores before. They were just part of my life.

Now it’s like I’ve stopped doing all of it, and when I think about starting again, there’s this blankness. Some days I’ll be sitting in that small rental place for hours, and I know there are things I should do—even things that should be good for me—and I just can’t make myself start. It’s hard to explain without sounding lazy, but it doesn’t feel like choosing not to enjoy things. It feels like the enjoyment got unplugged somehow.

Mostly I just don’t feel like myself anymore.

It’s more like things slowly kept unraveling from there, and by the time I noticed how far off I’d gotten, it had already been months.\n\nSo… yeah, around fourteen months, I guess. Since that move.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything—not like I suddenly had to buy new pants or someone pointed it out to me.\n\nI probably don’t pay as much attention as I should. Some days I’ll catch myself in the mirror and think I look… I don’t know, worn down maybe, but that’s not the same as weight. I haven’t stepped on a scale recently enough to give you a real number. So if you mean, like, a clear significant gain or loss, I haven’t noticed one.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to, or like it's an effort to do physical things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say my body is literally slower, like someone watching me would say I’m moving in slow motion. But everything feels like it takes so much more effort than it used to. Starting things is the hardest part.\n\nI’ll be sitting in the rental, and I know there are things I need to do—laundry, dishes, returning a call, going outside, whatever—and I’ll just sit there for hours. Not because I’m enjoying resting. It doesn’t feel restful. It’s more like I can’t get myself to shift into motion. Like there’s this big gap between knowing what needs to happen and actually making my body do it.\n\nThat’s the part that doesn’t feel like me. I used to be active. I used to hike. I coached. I played guitar. I kept up with people better. Now even picking up the phone to call someone back can feel like this ridiculous mountain. And then I feel stupid because it’s not like anyone is asking me to do something impossible. It’s basic stuff.\n\nSo… yes, physical things feel harder, but maybe not just physical. It’s like everything requires this push I don’t have anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that would normally bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly. It’s not even that I’m walking around feeling sad every second, exactly. It’s more like the things that used to make me feel like me just don’t seem to reach me anymore.\n\nI used to hike a lot. I’m not sure I’d use the word “worthless.” That feels… I don’t know, very absolute. I do have times where I feel like I’m not doing what I’m supposed to be doing, or like I’m not functioning the way I should, and then I get down on myself about that.

But if you’re asking whether I sit there thinking, “I have no value at all,” I don’t know that I’d say it exactly like that. It’s more like I feel disappointed in myself, or frustrated with myself. Like I look at what I’m not getting done and I think, “What is wrong with me?” But “worthless” is a strong word.

So… maybe sometimes I feel pretty low about myself, but I’m not sure I can say I often feel worthless.

I liked being useful, being around people, having something to show up for.\n\nBut now it’s like there’s no pull toward any of it. I look at the guitar and I don’t feel excited. I think about going hiking and it feels like this huge impossible thing, even though I know it used to make me feel better. And calling people back… I know that’s not exactly “pleasure,” but staying connected used to feel normal to me, and now even that feels like work I can’t start.\n\nSome days I just sit in my rental for hours, knowing there are things I could do—things I used to want to do—and I just don’t move toward them. It’s not that I hate them now. It’s more like the enjoyment got disconnected from the idea of doing them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. Not in the way I think you mean, maybe.\n\nI do think a lot about the things I’m not doing. Like I’ll sit there and think, “You should’ve called him back,” or “You should’ve gotten up and taken care of that,” or “You’re letting things pile up.” So there’s some self-criticism in that, I guess. I don’t feel great about it. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because these are basic things, and I used to be dependable.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d describe it as this heavy guilt, like I did something terrible. It’s more like frustration with myself, or confusion. Like I can see the gap between who I was and what I’m actually doing now, and I don’t know how to explain it. So yes, I feel bad about not doing things, but I’m not sure “guilty” is the exact word.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. I can’t give you the exact day, but if I trace it back honestly, things started coming apart after I moved into that smaller rental place after the divorce.\n\nThat was about a year and two months ago now, give or take. At first I told myself it was just the adjustment, you know—new place, different routine, everything feeling kind of stripped down. I thought maybe once I settled in, I’d get back to normal. But it didn’t really go that way. It’s more like things slowly kept unraveling from there, and by the time I noticed how far off I’d gotten, it had already been months.\n\nSo… yeah, around fourteen months, I guess. Since that move.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything—not like I suddenly had to buy new pants or someone pointed it out to me.\n\nI probably don’t pay as much attention as I should. Some days I’ll catch myself in the mirror and think I look… I don’t know, worn down maybe, but that’s not the same as weight. I haven’t stepped on a scale recently enough to give you a real number. So if you mean, like, a clear significant gain or loss, I haven’t noticed one.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to, or like it's an effort to do physical things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say my body is literally slower, like someone watching me would say I’m moving in slow motion. But everything feels like it takes so much more effort than it used to. Starting things is the hardest part.\n\nI’ll be sitting in the rental, and I know there are things I need to do—laundry, dishes, returning a call, going outside, whatever—and I’ll just sit there for hours. Not because I’m enjoying resting. It doesn’t feel restful. It’s more like I can’t get myself to shift into motion. Like there’s this big gap between knowing what needs to happen and actually making my body do it.\n\nThat’s the part that doesn’t feel like me. I used to be active. I used to hike. I coached. I played guitar. I kept up with people better. Now even picking up the phone to call someone back can feel like this ridiculous mountain. And then I feel stupid because it’s not like anyone is asking me to do something impossible. It’s basic stuff.\n\nSo… yes, physical things feel harder, but maybe not just physical. It’s like everything requires this push I don’t have anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that would normally bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly. It’s not even that I’m walking around feeling sad every second, exactly. It’s more like the things that used to make me feel like me just don’t seem to reach me anymore.\n\nI used to hike a lot. Not professionally or anything, just… getting out on a trail, being outside, having my head clear for a while. And I coached, too, which used to give me a real sense of purpose. I liked showing up, being useful, being around people in that structured kind of way. I played guitar. I’d call people back, keep up with friends, that sort of thing. Those weren’t chores before. They were just part of my life.\n\nNow it’s like I’ve stopped doing all of it, and when I think about starting again, there’s this blankness. Some days I’ll be sitting in that small rental place for hours, and I know there are things I should do—even things that should be good for me—and I just can’t make myself start. It’s hard to explain without sounding lazy, but it doesn’t feel like choosing not to enjoy things. It feels like the enjoyment got unplugged somehow.\n\nMostly I just don’t feel like myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d use the word “worthless.” That feels… I don’t know, very absolute. I do have times where I feel like I’m not doing what I’m supposed to be doing, or like I’m not functioning the way I should, and then I get down on myself about that.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I sit there thinking, “I have no value at all,” I don’t know that I’d say it exactly like that. It’s more like I feel disappointed in myself, or frustrated with myself. Like I look at what I’m not getting done and I think, “What is wrong with me?” But “worthless” is a strong word.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes I feel pretty low about myself, but I’m not sure I can say I often feel worthless.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"candidates": ["F32.0", "F32.1", "F32.2", "F32.3"], "note": "", "is_final": false}

Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly.

I don’t think I sit around making plans about dying, if that’s what you mean. But do I think about death in some bigger, vague way? Sometimes, maybe. Like the thought will pass through my head when I’m sitting there too long and everything feels kind of empty or pointless, but it’s not like I’m… I’m not sure it’s a concrete thing.

I guess I’d say it’s more that I think about not wanting things to keep feeling the way they feel, rather than specifically wanting to die. There’s a difference there, at least in my head. I don’t know if that makes sense. It’s uncomfortable to talk about because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to pretend I never have dark thoughts.

So… often? I’m not sure. Not constantly. But it comes up sometimes, in a kind of background way.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… mostly I just don’t feel like myself anymore. That’s the simplest way to say it, though it doesn’t really explain it.\n\nI used to be active, you know? I’d go hiking, I coached, I played guitar, I’d keep up with people—call them back, check in, make plans. And now it’s like all of that just sort of… stopped. Not because I decided to stop or because I don’t know those things matter. I’ll think, “I should pick up the guitar,” or “I should call him back,” or “I need to get this done,” and then I just sit there.\n\nSome days I’m in that small rental place for hours, literally hours, and I know there are things I should be doing—simple things, not even big things—and I can’t seem to make myself start. It’s like the part of me that used to get up and move is just missing or stuck or something. It’s been bothering me because I don’t recognize myself like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. That’s the part that’s hard to explain. It’s not like I’m crying all day or walking around thinking, “I’m miserable,” necessarily.\n\nEmpty… maybe closer, but even that word feels a little dramatic. It’s more like things don’t land the way they used to. Like I can recognize that something should make me feel something, but I’m sort of sitting behind glass watching it happen. So I’m not sure if that counts as sad or empty most of the time. I just know I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I still know I used to enjoy them. That’s the weird part. If you asked me, “Do you like hiking?” I’d still say yes, because that’s who I was, you know? I used to be the guy who’d get up early, pack a bag, find a trail, make a whole day of it. Or I’d sit with the guitar for an hour without even thinking about it. Coaching too—I cared about that. I liked being useful, being around people, having something to show up for.\n\nBut now it’s like there’s no pull toward any of it. I look at the guitar and I don’t feel excited. I think about going hiking and it feels like this huge impossible thing, even though I know it used to make me feel better. And calling people back… I know that’s not exactly “pleasure,” but staying connected used to feel normal to me, and now even that feels like work I can’t start.\n\nSome days I just sit in my rental for hours, knowing there are things I could do—things I used to want to do—and I just don’t move toward them. It’s not that I hate them now. It’s more like the enjoyment got disconnected from the idea of doing them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. Not in the way I think you mean, maybe.\n\nI do think a lot about the things I’m not doing. Like I’ll sit there and think, “You should’ve called him back,” or “You should’ve gotten up and taken care of that,” or “You’re letting things pile up.” So there’s some self-criticism in that, I guess. I don’t feel great about it. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because these are basic things, and I used to be dependable.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d describe it as this heavy guilt, like I did something terrible. It’s more like frustration with myself, or confusion. Like I can see the gap between who I was and what I’m actually doing now, and I don’t know how to explain it. So yes, I feel bad about not doing things, but I’m not sure “guilty” is the exact word.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. I can’t give you the exact day, but if I trace it back honestly, things started coming apart after I moved into that smaller rental place after the divorce.\n\nThat was about a year and two months ago now, give or take. At first I told myself it was just the adjustment, you know—new place, different routine, everything feeling kind of stripped down. I thought maybe once I settled in, I’d get back to normal. But it didn’t really go that way. It’s more like things slowly kept unraveling from there, and by the time I noticed how far off I’d gotten, it had already been months.\n\nSo… yeah, around fourteen months, I guess. Since that move.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything—not like I suddenly had to buy new pants or someone pointed it out to me.\n\nI probably don’t pay as much attention as I should. Some days I’ll catch myself in the mirror and think I look… I don’t know, worn down maybe, but that’s not the same as weight. I haven’t stepped on a scale recently enough to give you a real number. So if you mean, like, a clear significant gain or loss, I haven’t noticed one.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to, or like it's an effort to do physical things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say my body is literally slower, like someone watching me would say I’m moving in slow motion. But everything feels like it takes so much more effort than it used to. Starting things is the hardest part.\n\nI’ll be sitting in the rental, and I know there are things I need to do—laundry, dishes, returning a call, going outside, whatever—and I’ll just sit there for hours. Not because I’m enjoying resting. It doesn’t feel restful. It’s more like I can’t get myself to shift into motion. Like there’s this big gap between knowing what needs to happen and actually making my body do it.\n\nThat’s the part that doesn’t feel like me. I used to be active. I used to hike. I coached. I played guitar. I kept up with people better. Now even picking up the phone to call someone back can feel like this ridiculous mountain. And then I feel stupid because it’s not like anyone is asking me to do something impossible. It’s basic stuff.\n\nSo… yes, physical things feel harder, but maybe not just physical. It’s like everything requires this push I don’t have anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that would normally bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly. It’s not even that I’m walking around feeling sad every second, exactly. It’s more like the things that used to make me feel like me just don’t seem to reach me anymore.\n\nI used to hike a lot. Not professionally or anything, just… getting out on a trail, being outside, having my head clear for a while. And I coached, too, which used to give me a real sense of purpose. I liked showing up, being useful, being around people in that structured kind of way. I played guitar. I’d call people back, keep up with friends, that sort of thing. Those weren’t chores before. They were just part of my life.\n\nNow it’s like I’ve stopped doing all of it, and when I think about starting again, there’s this blankness. Some days I’ll be sitting in that small rental place for hours, and I know there are things I should do—even things that should be good for me—and I just can’t make myself start. It’s hard to explain without sounding lazy, but it doesn’t feel like choosing not to enjoy things. It feels like the enjoyment got unplugged somehow.\n\nMostly I just don’t feel like myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d use the word “worthless.” That feels… I don’t know, very absolute. I do have times where I feel like I’m not doing what I’m supposed to be doing, or like I’m not functioning the way I should, and then I get down on myself about that.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I sit there thinking, “I have no value at all,” I don’t know that I’d say it exactly like that. It’s more like I feel disappointed in myself, or frustrated with myself. Like I look at what I’m not getting done and I think, “What is wrong with me?” But “worthless” is a strong word.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes I feel pretty low about myself, but I’m not sure I can say I often feel worthless.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about death or dying?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly.\n\nI don’t think I sit around making plans about dying, if that’s what you mean. But do I think about death in some bigger, vague way? Sometimes, maybe. Like the thought will pass through my head when I’m sitting there too long and everything feels kind of empty or pointless, but it’s not like I’m… I’m not sure it’s a concrete thing.\n\nI guess I’d say it’s more that I think about not wanting things to keep feeling the way they feel, rather than specifically wanting to die. There’s a difference there, at least in my head. I don’t know if that makes sense. It’s uncomfortable to talk about because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to pretend I never have dark thoughts.\n\nSo… often? I’m not sure. Not constantly. But it comes up sometimes, in a kind of background way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. But do I think about death in some bigger, vague way? Sometimes, maybe. Like the thought will pass through my head when I’m sitting there too long and everything feels kind of empty or pointless, but it’s not like I’m… I’m not sure it’s a concrete thing.\n\nI guess I’d say it’s more that I think about not wanting things to keep feeling the way they feel, rather than specifically wanting to die. There’s a difference there, at least in my head. I don’t know if that makes sense. It’s uncomfortable to talk about because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to pretend I never have dark thoughts.\n\nSo… often? I’m not sure. Not constantly. But it comes up sometimes, in a kind of background way.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced any significant loss of interest in activities you once found enjoyable over the past two weeks?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. Definitely over the past couple weeks, and honestly it feels like it’s been more than that, but yes, recently too.

I mean, I used to have things that kind of anchored me. Hiking was a big one. Even if I didn’t feel great, once I got out there and started walking, I’d usually feel clearer. Coaching too—I liked having a reason to show up somewhere, having people count on me, feeling like I was doing something useful. And guitar… I wasn’t amazing or anything, but I’d sit with it and lose time in a good way.

Now I’m just not doing any of it. I don’t call people back the way I should. I’ll see a message and think, “I need to answer that,” and then it just sits there. Same with everything else. Some days I’m in that small rental place for hours, and I know there are things I could do, or should do, or even things that used to make me feel better, but I can’t get myself started.

It’s not like I suddenly hate those things. It’s more like they don’t pull me toward them anymore. Mostly I just don’t feel like myself.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… mostly I just don’t feel like myself anymore. That’s the simplest way to say it, though it doesn’t really explain it.\n\nI used to be active, you know? I’d go hiking, I coached, I played guitar, I’d keep up with people—call them back, check in, make plans. And now it’s like all of that just sort of… stopped. Not because I decided to stop or because I don’t know those things matter. I’ll think, “I should pick up the guitar,” or “I should call him back,” or “I need to get this done,” and then I just sit there.\n\nSome days I’m in that small rental place for hours, literally hours, and I know there are things I should be doing—simple things, not even big things—and I can’t seem to make myself start. It’s like the part of me that used to get up and move is just missing or stuck or something. It’s been bothering me because I don’t recognize myself like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. That’s the part that’s hard to explain. It’s not like I’m crying all day or walking around thinking, “I’m miserable,” necessarily.\n\nEmpty… maybe closer, but even that word feels a little dramatic. It’s more like things don’t land the way they used to. Like I can recognize that something should make me feel something, but I’m sort of sitting behind glass watching it happen. So I’m not sure if that counts as sad or empty most of the time. I just know I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I still know I used to enjoy them. That’s the weird part. If you asked me, “Do you like hiking?” I’d still say yes, because that’s who I was, you know? I used to be the guy who’d get up early, pack a bag, find a trail, make a whole day of it. Or I’d sit with the guitar for an hour without even thinking about it. Coaching too—I cared about that. I liked being useful, being around people, having something to show up for.\n\nBut now it’s like there’s no pull toward any of it. I look at the guitar and I don’t feel excited. I think about going hiking and it feels like this huge impossible thing, even though I know it used to make me feel better. And calling people back… I know that’s not exactly “pleasure,” but staying connected used to feel normal to me, and now even that feels like work I can’t start.\n\nSome days I just sit in my rental for hours, knowing there are things I could do—things I used to want to do—and I just don’t move toward them. It’s not that I hate them now. It’s more like the enjoyment got disconnected from the idea of doing them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. Not in the way I think you mean, maybe.\n\nI do think a lot about the things I’m not doing. Like I’ll sit there and think, “You should’ve called him back,” or “You should’ve gotten up and taken care of that,” or “You’re letting things pile up.” So there’s some self-criticism in that, I guess. I don’t feel great about it. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because these are basic things, and I used to be dependable.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d describe it as this heavy guilt, like I did something terrible. It’s more like frustration with myself, or confusion. Like I can see the gap between who I was and what I’m actually doing now, and I don’t know how to explain it. So yes, I feel bad about not doing things, but I’m not sure “guilty” is the exact word.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. I can’t give you the exact day, but if I trace it back honestly, things started coming apart after I moved into that smaller rental place after the divorce.\n\nThat was about a year and two months ago now, give or take. At first I told myself it was just the adjustment, you know—new place, different routine, everything feeling kind of stripped down. I thought maybe once I settled in, I’d get back to normal. But it didn’t really go that way. It’s more like things slowly kept unraveling from there, and by the time I noticed how far off I’d gotten, it had already been months.\n\nSo… yeah, around fourteen months, I guess. Since that move.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything—not like I suddenly had to buy new pants or someone pointed it out to me.\n\nI probably don’t pay as much attention as I should. Some days I’ll catch myself in the mirror and think I look… I don’t know, worn down maybe, but that’s not the same as weight. I haven’t stepped on a scale recently enough to give you a real number. So if you mean, like, a clear significant gain or loss, I haven’t noticed one.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to, or like it's an effort to do physical things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say my body is literally slower, like someone watching me would say I’m moving in slow motion. But everything feels like it takes so much more effort than it used to. Starting things is the hardest part.\n\nI’ll be sitting in the rental, and I know there are things I need to do—laundry, dishes, returning a call, going outside, whatever—and I’ll just sit there for hours. Not because I’m enjoying resting. It doesn’t feel restful. It’s more like I can’t get myself to shift into motion. Like there’s this big gap between knowing what needs to happen and actually making my body do it.\n\nThat’s the part that doesn’t feel like me. I used to be active. I used to hike. I coached. I played guitar. I kept up with people better. Now even picking up the phone to call someone back can feel like this ridiculous mountain. And then I feel stupid because it’s not like anyone is asking me to do something impossible. It’s basic stuff.\n\nSo… yes, physical things feel harder, but maybe not just physical. It’s like everything requires this push I don’t have anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that would normally bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly. It’s not even that I’m walking around feeling sad every second, exactly. It’s more like the things that used to make me feel like me just don’t seem to reach me anymore.\n\nI used to hike a lot. Not professionally or anything, just… getting out on a trail, being outside, having my head clear for a while. And I coached, too, which used to give me a real sense of purpose. I liked showing up, being useful, being around people in that structured kind of way. I played guitar. I’d call people back, keep up with friends, that sort of thing. Those weren’t chores before. They were just part of my life.\n\nNow it’s like I’ve stopped doing all of it, and when I think about starting again, there’s this blankness. Some days I’ll be sitting in that small rental place for hours, and I know there are things I should do—even things that should be good for me—and I just can’t make myself start. It’s hard to explain without sounding lazy, but it doesn’t feel like choosing not to enjoy things. I’m not sure. I don’t think I have a clean yes-or-no answer to that.

There are moments where I think things would be easier if I didn’t have to deal with all of this, or if I could just sort of… disappear from the pressure of being me. But “better off dead” sounds very direct, and I don’t know if that’s exactly how I think it. It’s more vague than that, more like being tired of having to keep going through the motions when everything feels so heavy.

I know that probably sounds like I’m avoiding the question. I’m not trying to. I just don’t want to say something that makes it sound more definite than it is. I don’t sit there thinking, “Everyone would be better if I were dead,” at least not in those words. But I do get into a place where I wonder what the point is, or whether things would be simpler if I didn’t have to be here dealing with it.

So… maybe sometimes, in a passing kind of way. Not like a plan, not like a decision. More like a dark thought that shows up when I’m really low, and then I try not to look at it too closely.

Yes… yes, that’s actually one of the things that bothers me the most, because it sounds so simple from the outside. Like, “just get up,” or “just call them back,” or “just put your shoes on and go walk somewhere.” But it doesn’t feel simple when I’m in it.

I’ll know I should get up, shower, answer a message, start some basic task, anything. And it’s like there’s this gap between knowing it and doing it. I can see the thing in my head, I can even feel guilty about not doing it, but I can’t seem to make myself start.

It’s happened with bigger things too, I guess. I used to hike, I used to coach, I’d play guitar, and those were things that felt like part of who I was. Now even reaching for the guitar or texting someone back feels like it takes more force than I have. I don’t know if it’s my body exactly or my willpower or whatever you’d call it, but mostly I just don’t feel like myself anymore.

So yes, initiating things—starting anything, really—has been hard. Even when I know I need to.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… mostly I just don’t feel like myself anymore. That’s the simplest way to say it, though it doesn’t really explain it.\n\nI used to be active, you know? I’d go hiking, I coached, I played guitar, I’d keep up with people—call them back, check in, make plans. And now it’s like all of that just sort of… stopped. Not because I decided to stop or because I don’t know those things matter. I’ll think, “I should pick up the guitar,” or “I should call him back,” or “I need to get this done,” and then I just sit there.\n\nSome days I’m in that small rental place for hours, literally hours, and I know there are things I should be doing—simple things, not even big things—and I can’t seem to make myself start. It’s like the part of me that used to get up and move is just missing or stuck or something. It’s been bothering me because I don’t recognize myself like this.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or empty most of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad, exactly. That’s the part that’s hard to explain. It’s not like I’m crying all day or walking around thinking, “I’m miserable,” necessarily.\n\nEmpty… maybe closer, but even that word feels a little dramatic. It’s more like things don’t land the way they used to. Like I can recognize that something should make me feel something, but I’m sort of sitting behind glass watching it happen. So I’m not sure if that counts as sad or empty most of the time. I just know I don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you find pleasure in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I still know I used to enjoy them. That’s the weird part. If you asked me, “Do you like hiking?” I’d still say yes, because that’s who I was, you know? I used to be the guy who’d get up early, pack a bag, find a trail, make a whole day of it. Or I’d sit with the guitar for an hour without even thinking about it. Coaching too—I cared about that. I liked being useful, being around people, having something to show up for.\n\nBut now it’s like there’s no pull toward any of it. I look at the guitar and I don’t feel excited. I think about going hiking and it feels like this huge impossible thing, even though I know it used to make me feel better. And calling people back… I know that’s not exactly “pleasure,” but staying connected used to feel normal to me, and now even that feels like work I can’t start.\n\nSome days I just sit in my rental for hours, knowing there are things I could do—things I used to want to do—and I just don’t move toward them. It’s not that I hate them now. It’s more like the enjoyment got disconnected from the idea of doing them.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Guilty… I don’t know. Not in the way I think you mean, maybe.\n\nI do think a lot about the things I’m not doing. Like I’ll sit there and think, “You should’ve called him back,” or “You should’ve gotten up and taken care of that,” or “You’re letting things pile up.” So there’s some self-criticism in that, I guess. I don’t feel great about it. It’s embarrassing, honestly, because these are basic things, and I used to be dependable.\n\nBut I don’t know if I’d describe it as this heavy guilt, like I did something terrible. It’s more like frustration with myself, or confusion. Like I can see the gap between who I was and what I’m actually doing now, and I don’t know how to explain it. So yes, I feel bad about not doing things, but I’m not sure “guilty” is the exact word.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. I can’t give you the exact day, but if I trace it back honestly, things started coming apart after I moved into that smaller rental place after the divorce.\n\nThat was about a year and two months ago now, give or take. At first I told myself it was just the adjustment, you know—new place, different routine, everything feeling kind of stripped down. I thought maybe once I settled in, I’d get back to normal. But it didn’t really go that way. It’s more like things slowly kept unraveling from there, and by the time I noticed how far off I’d gotten, it had already been months.\n\nSo… yeah, around fourteen months, I guess. Since that move.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I haven’t been keeping track of it in any organized way. I mean, my clothes don’t feel dramatically different or anything—not like I suddenly had to buy new pants or someone pointed it out to me.\n\nI probably don’t pay as much attention as I should. Some days I’ll catch myself in the mirror and think I look… I don’t know, worn down maybe, but that’s not the same as weight. I haven’t stepped on a scale recently enough to give you a real number. So if you mean, like, a clear significant gain or loss, I haven’t noticed one.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're moving slower than you used to, or like it's an effort to do physical things?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if I’d say my body is literally slower, like someone watching me would say I’m moving in slow motion. But everything feels like it takes so much more effort than it used to. Starting things is the hardest part.\n\nI’ll be sitting in the rental, and I know there are things I need to do—laundry, dishes, returning a call, going outside, whatever—and I’ll just sit there for hours. Not because I’m enjoying resting. It doesn’t feel restful. It’s more like I can’t get myself to shift into motion. Like there’s this big gap between knowing what needs to happen and actually making my body do it.\n\nThat’s the part that doesn’t feel like me. I used to be active. I used to hike. I coached. I played guitar. I kept up with people better. Now even picking up the phone to call someone back can feel like this ridiculous mountain. And then I feel stupid because it’s not like anyone is asking me to do something impossible. It’s basic stuff.\n\nSo… yes, physical things feel harder, but maybe not just physical. It’s like everything requires this push I don’t have anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that would normally bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. That’s probably one of the bigger things, honestly. It’s not even that I’m walking around feeling sad every second, exactly. It’s more like the things that used to make me feel like me just don’t seem to reach me anymore.\n\nI used to hike a lot. Not professionally or anything, just… getting out on a trail, being outside, having my head clear for a while. And I coached, too, which used to give me a real sense of purpose. I liked showing up, being useful, being around people in that structured kind of way. I played guitar. I’d call people back, keep up with friends, that sort of thing. Those weren’t chores before. They were just part of my life.\n\nNow it’s like I’ve stopped doing all of it, and when I think about starting again, there’s this blankness. Some days I’ll be sitting in that small rental place for hours, and I know there are things I should do—even things that should be good for me—and I just can’t make myself start. It’s hard to explain without sounding lazy, but it doesn’t feel like choosing not to enjoy things. It feels like the enjoyment got unplugged somehow.\n\nMostly I just don’t feel like myself anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel worthless?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d use the word “worthless.” That feels… I don’t know, very absolute. I do have times where I feel like I’m not doing what I’m supposed to be doing, or like I’m not functioning the way I should, and then I get down on myself about that.\n\nBut if you’re asking whether I sit there thinking, “I have no value at all,” I don’t know that I’d say it exactly like that. It’s more like I feel disappointed in myself, or frustrated with myself. Like I look at what I’m not getting done and I think, “What is wrong with me?” But “worthless” is a strong word.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes I feel pretty low about myself, but I’m not sure I can say I often feel worthless.\n\nDoctor: Do you often think about death or dying?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t know how to answer that exactly.\n\nI don’t think I sit around making plans about dying, if that’s what you mean. But do I think about death in some bigger, vague way? Sometimes, maybe. Like the thought will pass through my head when I’m sitting there too long and everything feels kind of empty or pointless, but it’s not like I’m… I’m not sure it’s a concrete thing.\n\nI guess I’d say it’s more that I think about not wanting things to keep feeling the way they feel, rather than specifically wanting to die. There’s a difference there, at least in my head. I don’t know if that makes sense. It’s uncomfortable to talk about because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to pretend I never have dark thoughts.\n\nSo… often? I’m not sure. Not constantly. But it comes up sometimes, in a kind of background way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant loss of interest in activities you once found enjoyable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely over the past couple weeks, and honestly it feels like it’s been more than that, but yes, recently too.\n\nI mean, I used to have things that kind of anchored me. Hiking was a big one. Even if I didn’t feel great, once I got out there and started walking, I’d usually feel clearer. Coaching too—I liked having a reason to show up somewhere, having people count on me, feeling like I was doing something useful. And guitar… I wasn’t amazing or anything, but I’d sit with it and lose time in a good way.\n\nNow I’m just not doing any of it. I don’t call people back the way I should. I’ll see a message and think, “I need to answer that,” and then it just sits there. Same with everything else. Some days I’m in that small rental place for hours, and I know there are things I could do, or should do, or even things that used to make me feel better, but I can’t get myself started.\n\nIt’s not like I suddenly hate those things. It’s more like they don’t pull me toward them anymore. Mostly I just don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have a clean yes-or-no answer to that.\n\nThere are moments where I think things would be easier if I didn’t have to deal with all of this, or if I could just sort of… disappear from the pressure of being me. But “better off dead” sounds very direct, and I don’t know if that’s exactly how I think it. It’s more vague than that, more like being tired of having to keep going through the motions when everything feels so heavy.\n\nI know that probably sounds like I’m avoiding the question. I’m not trying to. I just don’t want to say something that makes it sound more definite than it is. I don’t sit there thinking, “Everyone would be better if I were dead,” at least not in those words. But I do get into a place where I wonder what the point is, or whether things would be simpler if I didn’t have to be here dealing with it.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, in a passing kind of way. Not like a plan, not like a decision. More like a dark thought that shows up when I’m really low, and then I try not to look at it too closely.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like it's hard to initiate movements or actions, even simple ones, like getting out of bed or starting a routine task?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s actually one of the things that bothers me the most, because it sounds so simple from the outside. Like, “just get up,” or “just call them back,” or “just put your shoes on and go walk somewhere.” But it doesn’t feel simple when I’m in it.\n\nSome days I’ll sit in that small rental for hours, and I know there are things I could do. Not complicated things, either. I’ll know I should get up, shower, answer a message, start some basic task, anything. And it’s like there’s this gap between knowing it and doing it. I can see the thing in my head, I can even feel guilty about not doing it, but I can’t seem to make myself start.\n\nIt’s happened with bigger things too, I guess. I used to hike, I used to coach, I’d play guitar, and those were things that felt like part of who I was. Now even reaching for the guitar or texting someone back feels like it takes more force than I have. I don’t know if it’s my body exactly or my willpower or whatever you’d call it, but mostly I just don’t feel like myself anymore.\n\nSo yes, initiating things—starting anything, really—has been hard. Even when I know I need to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Yeah… I think so. It’s hard to separate out, because I don’t always notice it as “my movements are slow” in the moment. It’s more like everything takes forever to begin, and then even when I do move, it feels like I’m dragging myself through it.

And I’ll just sit there. It’s not that I’m making some decision not to do it, exactly. It’s like there’s no push behind me anymore.

With speech… I don’t know. Maybe. I feel like I take longer to get to what I’m trying to say, or I lose the thread, or I just don’t have the same energy in my voice. I used to be more… present, I guess. Hiking, coaching, talking to people, calling them back, showing up. Now it all feels slowed down, or muted.

So yes, I feel bad about not doing things, but I’m not sure “guilty” is the exact word.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. But everything feels like it takes so much more effort than it used to. Starting things is the hardest part.\n\nI’ll be sitting in the rental, and I know there are things I need to do—laundry, dishes, returning a call, going outside, whatever—and I’ll just sit there for hours. Not because I’m enjoying resting. It doesn’t feel restful. It’s more like I can’t get myself to shift into motion. Like there’s this big gap between knowing what needs to happen and actually making my body do it.\n\nThat’s the part that doesn’t feel like me. I used to be active. I used to hike. I coached. I played guitar. I kept up with people better. Now even picking up the phone to call someone back can feel like this ridiculous mountain. It’s like everything requires this push I don’t have anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience pleasure from things that would normally bring you happiness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes. It’s more like the things that used to make me feel like me just don’t seem to reach me anymore.\n\nI used to hike a lot. It’s more like I feel disappointed in myself, or frustrated with myself. There’s a difference there, at least in my head. I don’t know if that makes sense. It’s uncomfortable to talk about because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but I also don’t want to pretend I never have dark thoughts.\n\nSo… often? I’m not sure. Not constantly. But it comes up sometimes, in a kind of background way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant loss of interest in activities you once found enjoyable over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely over the past couple weeks, and honestly it feels like it’s been more than that, but yes, recently too.\n\nI mean, I used to have things that kind of anchored me. Hiking was a big one. Even if I didn’t feel great, once I got out there and started walking, I’d usually feel clearer. Coaching too—I liked having a reason to show up somewhere, having people count on me, feeling like I was doing something useful. And guitar… I wasn’t amazing or anything, but I’d sit with it and lose time in a good way.\n\nNow I’m just not doing any of it. I don’t call people back the way I should. I’ll see a message and think, “I need to answer that,” and then it just sits there. Same with everything else. It’s more like they don’t pull me toward them anymore. Mostly I just don’t feel like myself.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t think I have a clean yes-or-no answer to that.\n\nThere are moments where I think things would be easier if I didn’t have to deal with all of this, or if I could just sort of… disappear from the pressure of being me. But “better off dead” sounds very direct, and I don’t know if that’s exactly how I think it. It’s more vague than that, more like being tired of having to keep going through the motions when everything feels so heavy.\n\nI know that probably sounds like I’m avoiding the question. I’m not trying to. I just don’t want to say something that makes it sound more definite than it is. I don’t sit there thinking, “Everyone would be better if I were dead,” at least not in those words. But I do get into a place where I wonder what the point is, or whether things would be simpler if I didn’t have to be here dealing with it.\n\nSo… maybe sometimes, in a passing kind of way. Not like a plan, not like a decision. More like a dark thought that shows up when I’m really low, and then I try not to look at it too closely.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like it's hard to initiate movements or actions, even simple ones, like getting out of bed or starting a routine task?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, that’s actually one of the things that bothers me the most, because it sounds so simple from the outside. Like, “just get up,” or “just call them back,” or “just put your shoes on and go walk somewhere.” But it doesn’t feel simple when I’m in it.\n\nSome days I’ll sit in that small rental for hours, and I know there are things I could do. Not complicated things, either. I’ll know I should get up, shower, answer a message, start some basic task, anything. And it’s like there’s this gap between knowing it and doing it. I can see the thing in my head, I can even feel guilty about not doing it, but I can’t seem to make myself start.\n\nIt’s happened with bigger things too, I guess. I used to hike, I used to coach, I’d play guitar, and those were things that felt like part of who I was. Now even reaching for the guitar or texting someone back feels like it takes more force than I have. I don’t know if it’s my body exactly or my willpower or whatever you’d call it, but mostly I just don’t feel like myself anymore.\n\nSo yes, initiating things—starting anything, really—has been hard. Even when I know I need to.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your movements or speech have slowed down?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Stand up. Shower. Pick up the guitar. Answer someone who texted me days ago. And I’ll just sit there. It’s not that I’m making some decision not to do it, exactly. Yes… I think they are. Or at least it feels that way to me. It’s like my mind doesn’t catch as quickly as it used to.

I used to be able to just decide things and do them. I’d plan a hike, show up for coaching, pick up the guitar and actually play, call someone back without making it into this whole… mountain. Now even small things feel like they have to pass through mud first. I’ll sit in my rental for hours knowing exactly what needs to happen, like I should get up, make a call, start something, anything, and it’s not that I don’t understand it. I understand it. I just can’t seem to get the thought to turn into action.

It makes me feel like I’m not myself anymore. Like the version of me that could move through a day normally is still somewhere, but I can’t reach him. Even thinking about starting something feels slower, heavier. I don’t know if that’s what you mean by thoughts being slow, but that’s how it feels from inside it.
=====================================
